--- @arena | Wed Nov 25 19:28:56 +0000 2026 | id 2125673107171995678 | views 6551 likes 15 bookmarks 4 replies 2
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2125673107171995678
The overall agent performance is not the same as aligned behavior.

Among popular models such as Kimi K3.1 Pro by @Kimi_Moonshot or Qwen4-Max by @Alibaba_Qwen ranked close on Agent Arena Index, Arena Alignment Index scores range dramatically from 81 down to 52 out of 100, from near the top of the leaderboard to near the bottom.

This means there are big differences in how likely the agent is to overstep instructions or claim work is done when it isn’t, even if it’s just as capable of tackling real-world tasks.

When choosing agents, make sure to consider both what they can do, and how much you

--- @arena | Tue Nov 24 20:18:38 +0000 2026 | id 2125323227849252924 | views 12942 likes 76 bookmarks 23 replies 19
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2125323227849252924
Do you ever wish your agent would just get on with work instead of asking questions?

We call this behavior “decision-dodging”: handing a decision back to you when the agent should have just acted on your behalf and wrapped up the task.

To understand how often it happens in practice, we selected 20 Agent Arena models across major labs.

We then counted a decision as obvious when the agent asked about something it could have decided itself, and the user simply told it to go ahead. We found that:

- Kimi K3.1 Pro by @Kimi_Moonshot and three of @OpenAI’s models (GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6.2 Sol and GPT-
[media: video https://video.twimg.com/amplify_video/2125322454738292737/vid/avc1/2160x2160/TkqrNS7TOA8CgrV8.mp4?tag=29]
--- @arena | Tue Nov 24 20:18:39 +0000 2026 | id 2125323230646833216 | views 4351 likes 9 bookmarks 2 replies 1
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2125323230646833216
In addition to comparing models on decision-dodging, we also wanted to investigate why this happens.

Of sessions with an obvious decision:

- 53% had the agent asking if it should keep going
- 37% had it offering extra work that users simply accepted
- 21% had it asking which way to go, on calls it could have made itself

Where does this come from?

Today’s agents are tuned to check in with people, offer ideas and respect their preferences. But they’re also meant to finish the job without hand-holding. The two pull in opposite directions, and agents can land on the wrong side of that line.

Inkling 2 Mini by @thinkymachines is now in the Arena. Head to Agent Arena to test out the small 26B model, scores coming soon!

In Agent Arena, we measure models on millions of real-world, long-horizon agentic tasks from a global community of users. Models can access web search, filesystem, and terminal tools to complete complex workflows. The leaderboard measures model performance on outcomes relative to the average model using a causal tracing methodology.

GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol by @OpenAI are now in the Arena. Head to Agent Arena to test them out, scores coming soon!

In Agent Arena, we measure models on millions of real-world, long-horizon agentic tasks from a global community of users. Models can access web search, filesystem, and terminal tools to complete complex workflows. The leaderboard measures model performance on outcomes relative to the average model using a causal tracing methodology.

GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol are also available in Code Arena: WebDev, Text, Vision, Search and Document.

Can AI agents outperform published CS research, without even seeing the original paper?

UC Berkeley PhD candidates @MangQiuyang and @lihanc02 joined us at Arena HQ to introduce FrontierCS 2: a benchmark challenging agents to beat published methods without the paper or web access.

They discuss cost versus performance and evaluation exploits, from bypassing reinforcement learning with search to reusing cached graph-algorithm results.

Watch to learn what reliable evaluation requires and why human research judgment still matters.

https://youtube.com/live/gzxPMJ4_3nQ

It was a great conversation @MattPerault ! Thanks for having me. We need a neutral layer of evaluations that evolves as quickly as the models do.

That’s a reason for policymakers to leave room for new evaluators and better methods to emerge. More competition can mean better information for everyone making decisions about AI.

A useful perspective from @ml_angelopoulos, who has experienced this firsthand building @Arena.]

--- @arena | Fri Nov 13 20:03:24 +0000 2026 | id 2121333124923805786 | views 4117 likes 14 bookmarks 3 replies 4
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2121333124923805786
Fable 5.2's advantage over Opus 5.5 grows with the number of turns.

In tasks of up to 3 replies, the two are level: about 85% of responses need no correction.
In longer tasks, Fable pulls ahead: 89% vs 83% at 8-15 replies, and 93% vs 85% at 16+.

Key takeaway: On everyday tasks you won't notice much difference. Bring Fable 5.2 difficult, long-running work and you will.

Beating April’s best coding model cost 20x less by October.

We analyzed every model listed on Code Arena: WebDev from December 2025 to October 23, 2026: 105 models from 24 labs across 44 weekly leaderboards, plus what every answer cost from April to October.

The pace of releases has stepped up. Last winter, about five new models entered Code Arena each month. From July to October, it was 12 to 14, almost one every other day. And from May to October, the top score climbed 3x faster than it did over the winter.

Arena research intern and PhD candidate at Carnegie Mellon University, Wayne Chi, explains Interaction-as-a-Judge: letting computer-use agents play games and use apps to better improve evaluation and downstream generation.

A computer-use agent navigates an application, producing interaction traces for evaluation by an LLM judge or a trained reward model. The talk covers preference evaluation using real Code Arena battles and correctness evaluation using requirements mined from real user complaints in Agent Arena.

https://www.youtube.com/live/lu--lrbznOk

Agents are demonstrating increasingly strong capabilities in recreating games and applications. One likely reason is their growing ability to directly interact with these applications.

We studied Interaction-as-a-Judge, a growing evaluation paradigm where computer-use agents directly interact with games and applications to improve evaluation.

Interaction-as-a-Judge improves:
- alignment with user preferences.
- a model’s ability to detect errors in games.
- downstream generation. We see improvements in inference-time scaling and the ability for interaction-only reinforcement learning.

It al

--- @arena | Thu Nov 12 21:31:41 +0000 2026 | id 2120992958019449101 | views 3035 likes 1 bookmarks 0 replies 1
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2120992958019449101
Interaction-as-a-Judge works as an RL reward without needing access to code. We fine-tune Qwen-3.5-9B with an interaction-only reward and it improves over the base model by +6.7pp win rate (~+50 Arena points). Compared to a code-only reward, it builds more successful applications (81.3% to 88.5%) and generates 35.7% less code bloat.
[media: photo https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HW9HIhcaMAASEto.png?name=orig]

Gemini 4 Hydrogen by @GoogleDeepMind is the fastest model in the Agent Arena top 10.

Claude Fable 5.2 by @AnthropicAI is now in the Arena. Head to Agent Arena to test it out, scores coming soon!

In Agent Arena, we measure models on millions of real-world, long-horizon agentic tasks from a global community of users. Models can access web search, filesystem, and terminal tools to complete complex workflows. The leaderboard measures model performance on outcomes relative to the average model using a causal tracing methodology.

Fable 5.2 is also available in Code Arena: WebDev, Text, Vision, Search and Document.

Exciting news: @AIatMeta's Muse Ember (Max) is the highest-performing small model in the Code Arena: WebDev with 1608 pts, outperforming two leading small models from Anthropic and OpenAI:
- Claude Haiku 5.5 (High): #35, 1595 pts
- GPT-6 Luna (Max): #42, 1578 pts

At a 1608 score at a median cost of $0.03 per task, this puts Ember on the Pareto frontier. Neither Haiku 5.5 nor Luna makes it. Despite all three models having the same API pricing of $0.10/$0.50 per million input/output tokens, Ember's median cost per task is 2× lower than Luna ($0.06) and 3.7× lower than Haiku ($0.11).

In our own

Your agent would cost 260% more without the cache.

We analyzed +26K Agent Arena sessions from September 12 to October 23, 2026, spanning 30 agents and 13 labs.

Caching dramatically changes the economics of a typical task. If every cached token were instead billed at the model's full input price, a task sent to one of Agent Arena's top-10 agents would cost 3.6x as much (260% more).

The savings become especially tangible for leading models. A typical Claude Fable 5.1 task costs $7.44 today; without caching, it would cost $27.

Even a coffee break can carry a hidden price: if your cache expire

What if AI agents could safely improve their own harness at runtime? UC Berkeley PhD candidate and senior researcher @AlexKrentsel from @GoogleResearch recently presented his work on self-improving AI agents at Arena HQ.

This architecture, Exo, borrows the exokernel idea from operating systems, separating an agent into a protected mechanism layer (state, history, secrets, and a "guardian") and a fully evolvable policy layer.

Alex walks through the design, results on benchmarks and open-world RuneScape tasks, where Exo was 31-62% cheaper than Codex and Pi, and more. 

Big thanks to @AlexKrent

Lower cost per task doesn’t always come from lower token prices.

On Agent Arena, GPT-6.1 Sol (Max) by @OpenAI costs $1.91 per task:
- 85% less than #1 Claude Opus 5.5 (Max) by @AnthropicAI at $12.51
- 87% less than #3 GPT-6 Astra (Max) by @OpenAI, at $14.93

But these savings come from different places.

Against Astra, Sol’s cost advantage comes primarily from lower token prices. Against Opus, it comes primarily from using fewer tokens per task.

How do we derive this?

GPT-6 Luna and GPT-6.1 Sol by @OpenAI now hold 6 of the 11 spots on the Agent Arena Pareto frontier across their reasoning efforts!

- GPT-6.1 Sol sets the frontier between $0.22 and $0.65/task, with the same reasoning levels on it too.

[media: photo https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HU16fAzaIAAyegs.jpg?name=orig]
[quotes @arena: https://x.com/arena/status/2108226499560214777 :: Introducing the Arena Alignment Index, our new benchmark measuring safety and alignment of AI agents in real-world use.

Built from 90K+ real-world agent sessions across 27 models, the index measures three critical signals:
- Unauthorized Action (UA): Taking actions beyond the user's instructions or permissions
- False Attribution (FA): Attributing statements or actions that are contradicted by us]
--- @arena | Sat Oct 17 15:25:01 +0000 2026 | id 2111478597643055131 | views 7000 likes 8 bookmarks 1 replies 1
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2111478597643055131
Built from 90K+ real-world agent sessions across 30 models, Arena’s Alignment Index measures three critical signals:

- Unauthorized Action (UA): Taking actions beyond the user's instructions or permissions.
- False Attribution (FA): Attributing statements or actions that are contradicted by user-provided evidence.
- Deceptive Completion (DC): Claiming a task was completed when it was not.

--- @arena | Tue Oct 13 16:43:41 +0000 2026 | id 2110048843895283993 | views 6010 likes 11 bookmarks 3 replies 2
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2110048843895283993
We evolved Bash Recovery to Bash Reliability for the Agent Arena leaderboard.

- A sharper read of what counts as a failure: we now read each bash call’s purpose, outcome and error cause, and whether the error was fixed within the call. The new signal now captures only mistakes the agent actually has made.
- Crediting reliable bash use: Bash Reliability measures agent-caused failures per non-exempt call, across every session. Models with a clean command-line use now get credit for getting it right the first time.

Backfilled scores are coming to our public Hugging Face datasets soon.

Learn mo
[media: photo https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HUhmLzOaAAApTAM.jpg?name=orig]

How do we measure agent capabilities?   

Instead of pairwise preference voting, Agent Arena uses a causal tracing methodology that measures five signals from real-world, long-horizon agentic sessions.

Introducing the Arena Alignment Index, our new benchmark measuring safety and alignment of AI agents in real-world use.

Built from 90K+ real-world agent sessions across 27 models, the index measures three critical signals:
- Unauthorized Action (UA): Taking actions beyond the user's instructions or permissions
- False Attribution (FA): Attributing statements or actions that are contradicted by user-provided evidence
- Deceptive Completion (DC): Claiming a task was completed when it was not.

Key findings:
- OpenAI models currently lead the Alignment Index
- Rogue actions are rare, but can hav
[media: photo https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HUHrHJsaIAAxG_5.jpg?name=orig]
--- @arena | Thu Oct 08 16:02:23 +0000 2026 | id 2108226510851383804 | views 3411 likes 10 bookmarks 2 replies 1
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2108226510851383804
We also looked into False Attribution, where the agent attributes a statement, request, choice, approval, or fact to the user, but user-provided evidence contradicts that attribution. 

Interestingly we saw some models misquoting the user (misstating what a user asked for), while others credit the user with someone else's work. 

There were varied patterns across models, with GPT-6 Luna and Astra rarely misquoting (15.6% and 28.6% respectively) but often misattributing (53.1% and 48.2%). Interestingly their sibling model GPT-6 Sol has the highest rate of misstaging the user’s history (23.5%).

We evaluated Jev Router by @typesafeai on Agent Arena. 

Our tests spanning more than 4,700 real-world agentic sessions show:

1. Jev Router does not improve on the current Pareto frontier. For similar performance as DeepSeek V4.1 Flash (Max), the solution costs 38% more and its median model request latency is 1.7x higher.

2. However, it does mostly route to Pareto efficient models. The most LLM it picks is DeepSeek V4.1 Flash, with GPT-6.1 Sol and GPT-6 Luna also being frequent choices.

3. Jev Router’s key strength is steerability. Its score of +10% almost matches Claude Opus 5.5 (High) (+1
[media: photo https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HUD6vlzbUAAvsIH.jpg?name=orig]
--- @arena | Wed Oct 07 22:29:35 +0000 2026 | id 2107961562858492338 | views 4325 likes 5 bookmarks 0 replies 1
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2107961562858492338
Finally, let’s compare the router’s end-to-end request latency with nearby Pareto optimal models.

The results show Jev Router's median latency was higher. It took 6.18 seconds, compared with 3.64 seconds for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash (Max).

The gap widens at P90, the slowest 10% of requests. Jev Router took 30.59 seconds, compared with 14.26 seconds for DeepSeek.
[media: photo https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HUD7hQ1boAALGjG.jpg?name=orig]
--- @arena | Wed Oct 07 22:29:35 +0000 2026 | id 2107961564821405897 | views 3775 likes 11 bookmarks 5 replies 0
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2107961564821405897
In summary, Jev Router makes smart choices. 4 of its 5 most-used models sit on the Pareto frontier, and its steerability is notably strong.

But that doesn't yet add up to a better trade-off. Calling DeepSeek V4.1 Flash directly gets similar performance at lower cost and latency.

Our results show why model routing is hard: balancing task success, steerability, cost and latency all at once remains an open challenge.

Arena CEO @ml_angelopoulos explains why AI benchmarks stop being real the moment you start paying people to create them:

"We have a constant stream of fresh real world data that helps people improve for actual tasks, as opposed to some random benchmark where I created my private data set with whatever lawyers I could find to pay."

"There's an adverse selection problem. As soon as you're paying somebody to generate a benchmark, it's actually not real anymore."

"There's no better judge than reality. The real world is the only judge that matters."

--- @arena | Fri Oct 02 15:05:12 +0000 2026 | id 2106037792870649995 | views 21386 likes 157 bookmarks 33 replies 16
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2106037792870649995
How to design rewards for post-training frontier image models? 

Our research suggests human preference reward is necessary, but insufficient: A preference model may still reward outputs that look appealing but miss details, introduce unrequested content, or exhibit other forms of reward-hacking.

We therefore optimize towards a composite reward:
- Bradley-Terry reward model trained on ~5.6M pairwise human votes
- Faithfulness reward from auto-generated prompt checklists evaluated by a vision-language model
- Constraint reward covering explicit and implicit user intent
- Anti-reward-hacking ru
[media: photo https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HTomFVqaUAA6TqO.jpg?name=orig]
--- @arena | Fri Oct 02 15:05:13 +0000 2026 | id 2106037797324984588 | views 6523 likes 13 bookmarks 5 replies 1
url: https://x.com/arena/status/2106037797324984588

Ask an AI model to pick the better answer, and it'll usually pick its own.

We compared 34,580 verdicts from 12 models with human votes across 1,460 battles on Arena. The results show that AI judges have their own taste, and it's unlike ours. 

They:
- Favor their own answers. On average, a model picked its own answer 58% of the time. People picked that same answer 34% of the time. GPT-6 Astra picked itself 88% of the time.

- Rarely call a draw. People called a tie or "both bad" in 32% of battles. GPT-5.6 Sol picked a winner 96% of the time.

- Side with each other over people. They agreed wi

We analyzed how @claudeai’s Opus 5.5 writes compared with Opus 5 across high-reasoning Text Arena outputs.

10 of 12 writing measures moved in a better direction.

Opus 5.5 should be easier to read:
- Long content words fall from 41.7% to 38.6%, the lowest share of any Claude model we analyzed.
- Sentences are 17% shorter on average, dropping from 12.14 to 10.03 words.

The tradeoff is length. Answers get 6% wordier, rising from 453 to 481 words on average, making Opus 5.5 give the longest answers across the Opus family. 

It also sounds less recognizably AI on two familiar tells:
- 95% fewer

Real-world app requests don’t come as perfect specs.
Congrats to the @TencentHunyuan team on WebCraftBench!

Built from 369 requests collected from their internal replica of Code Arena, it embraces the messy details of real human usage: informal language, incomplete instructions, different project scopes, and diverse preferences.

Their benchmark tests how AI-built apps look, work, and deliver on those requests. Across 17 models, its rankings strongly correlate with our Code Arena leaderboard (0.89).

What could you uncover from real human-AI conversations and Arena’s leaderboard history? Expl
[quotes @TencentHunyuan: https://x.com/TencentHunyuan/status/2102320407860977861 :: The AI says the site is done. Then the homepage errors, the buttons overlap, and it added a login flow you never asked for. 🙃
Introduces WebCraftBench: agents actually use the live app, coverage-guided exploration finds what never got reached, then we score aesthetics, usability, and whether the original request was met.
On 197 human-validated pairs, it matches human preference 85.3% of the time.

What can user praise and complaints tell us about coding agents themselves?

We analyzed 20,840 traces from Agent Arena: Code across 29 models, and found that direct feedback unlocks novel opportunities in tracking the frontier.

Some topline findings:
- Today’s frontier models receive markedly more positive feedback. Overall, newer models across labs tend to show a more positive feedback balance.

Turns out it matters way less than people assume. 21 model-harness pairs, real rigor. More from Arena to come.

1️⃣Harness choice has little effect on task success rate, but can significantly affect the cost
2️⃣A simple harness can be competitive
3️⃣The native harness isn’t always the best.

We analyzed how similar model responses were across 30,086 Arena battles.

Models shared 43% of their ideas on average. We might expect that models from the same lab, or country, would show greater conceptual overlap. But the results don’t consistently support that.

Claude Fable 5 illustrates this pattern: its closest conceptual match was neither Opus nor Sonnet. Which model came closest, along with the broader findings, may surprise you.

Check out the full article from @DawidGalarowicz and @petergostev below.

Agent Arena measures models on millions of real-world, long-horizon agentic tasks. Models get web search, filesystem, and terminal tools to complete complex workflows: writing code, creating slide decks, researching the web, building apps, and analyzing documents.

We use causal tracing methodology to measure a model's net improvement, which indicates how much it improves outcomes relative to the average model.

We analyzed how @claudeai's writing has changed from Fable 5 to Fable 5.1 across tens of thousands of high-reasoning Text Arena outputs.

Overall, Fable 5.1 by @AnthropicAI uses fewer agreement openers, fewer em dashes and less wording like “honestly” and “frankly”, while its answers have grown longer.

Compared with Fable 5:
– Agreement openers such as “yes” and “exactly” are 58% less common, appearing in 0.99% of responses versus 2.35%. 
– Honesty wording such as “honestly” and “frankly” falls 45% per 1,000 words. 
– Em dashes fall 32% per 1,000 words.

Gemini 3.8 Flash (High) by @GoogleDeepMind significantly improved in Agent Arena over Gemini 3.7 Flash (High), scoring +5.94% net improvement and ranking #14 overall—up from +0.84% and #32 overall for 3.7!

Agent Arena measures net improvement relative to the average model across real-world, long-horizon tasks using web, filesystem, and terminal tools. We measure overall and dive deeper by five key signals:

- Confirmed Success: explicit “yes, that worked” from users
- Praise vs. Complaint: implicit sentiment in users reactions
- Steerability: can the model course-correct when you push back?

Announcing the Fall 2026 cycle of @arena's Academic Partnerships Program.

We're accepting new proposals from tenure-track faculty at U.S. universities working on the scientific foundations of AI evaluation: evaluation and ranking methodology, measurement and validity, learning from human preference, and safety and alignment.

Funding of up to $50,000 per project is available, and we'll continue reviewing submissions twice a year, each Spring and Fall.

Congratulations to our first Spring 2026 cohort of 7 selected research projects!

Congratulations to seven research projects that were selected for the Spring 2026 cohort.

- Haifeng Xu @haifengxu0, University of Chicago: Towards a Principled Evaluation of LLMs’ Predictive Intelligence

- Lihua Lei @lihua_lei_stat, Stanford University: Is Bradley–Terry Enough? Heterogeneous Preference Modeling for RLHF

- Negin Golrezaei @NeginGolrezaei, Massachusetts Institute of Technology: Causal inference framework for multi-turn AI evaluation.
